Enforcement\n\nUse this reference when recommending principles and their checks.\nStart from the project's requirements, approved design and observed risks.\nThese examples are candidates, not adapter | Import boundary analysis resolving project paths and aliases | An allowed public interface import passes and a direct database import fails |\n| Write each rule once | Generated settings match the authoritative source | Deterministic regeneration and comparison | A stale generated value fails |\n| Validate external input | Reject invalid input at the actual public boundary | Behaviour tests and applicable schema or type checks | Invalid input is rejected and valid input succeeds |\n| Keep behaviour stable during cleanup | Existing supported journeys remain compatible | Relevant integration or end-to-end checks | The same supported journeys pass before and after the change |\n| Tests protect agreed behaviour | Tests call the public interface and take expected results from an approved source | Human review against the acceptance source | A test asserting private implementation, or an expectation copied from implementation output, is identified in review |\n| Keep dependencies purposeful | New abstractions serve a current use and a clear responsibility | Human design review against vision and technical design | Review identifies the requirement, responsibility and simpler alternative |\n\nDo not disguise a human design judgement as a deterministic violation.\nGive the reviewer the relevant authority, example and evidence instead.\n\n## Proof required before adopting an automated rule\n\nRequire a representative valid case and a deliberate violation in disposable fixtures.\nThe valid case must pass.\nThe violation must fail with the expected diagnostic and exit code.\nThe clean project must pass the selected final checks.\nUse the selected tool's native rule-testing mechanism where available.\nInclude relevant syntax, aliases, re-exports, generated code and documented exceptions in the proof scope.\nRecord unsupported syntax and dynamic dependencies as coverage limits rather than assuming they pass.\n\nAfter preparing native dependencies in the clean and independently seeded fixtures, run `rule-proof --repo <target> --check-plan <file> --check-id <id> --valid <clean-dir> --violation <seeded-dir> --expect <specific-diagnostic> --json`.\nThe plan follows `schemas/check-plan.json`; the selected check supplies the native child-command object.\nThe command runs separate disposable copies, records versions, exits and diagnostics in scratch, and removes the copies.\nRepeat for each private import, alias or equivalent resolution bypass and forbidden cycle, retaining public imports and permitted dependency direction in the clean control.\nUnavailable native tools, interrupted execution and failed clean setup are blocked, never proof.\n\nCapture the maintained command, tool version, output, exit code and relevant input state directly from execution.\nThe same maintained command must work locally and in the project's CI when the project selects one, without ignoring failures.\nFor behaviour compatibility, preserve agreed acceptance outcomes rather than proving only that unit checks are green.\nThese are evidence requirements for selected enforcement, not a development workflow.\n\n## Integrate the maintained command\n\nUse reviewed selected findings and `apply` to extend the project's existing check command rather than creating a competing entry point.\nThe target repo owns each checker or pins its declared development dependency; running checks must not require an installed bstack skill or agent session.\nScope each package rule in its native configuration or checker, and prove a sibling package's permitted use remains valid.\nApply the complete initial documented baseline through selected debt findings before expecting the audited project to pass.\nKeep every known violation visible with its reason and removal condition; new violations fail and fixed entries must be removed.\nContinue to prove each rule independently on clean fixtures without counting unrelated audited debt as new violations.\n\nFor integration proof, select the maintained command in the check plan, for example executable `npm`, args `[\"run\", \"check\"]`, cwd `.` and versionArgs `[\"--version\"]`.\nRun the same rule-proof interface against separate disposable sources containing the integrated command, installed native dependencies and the accepted baseline, with one new seeded violation in the negative source.\nCapture both exits and the specific new diagnostic; a tool setup failure does not prove enforcement.\nThe valid control must include a permitted sibling-package use where package scope matters.\n\nIf the project selects CI, apply the same maintained command to its configuration as another reviewed edit.\nAn edit that integrates selected checks declares optional `checkIntegration`, a nonempty list of unique command paths, each a nonempty array of string keys.\nFor `package.json`, mark each selected script with a path such as `[[\"scripts\", \"check\"]]`; other changed scripts receive no integration validation.\nFor a selected `.yml` or `.yaml` CI edit, use JSON syntax, which is valid YAML, and mark the exact `run` or `script` paths, such as `[[\"jobs\", \"check\", \"steps\", \"0\", \"run\"]]`.\nThe installed runtime supports only this JSON subset for marked CI edits; it uses Node built-ins and does not parse general YAML.\nEvery selected path must resolve to a string command in the complete proposed bytes; missing commands, deletion and unsupported marked formats fail before writes.\n`continue-on-error` and `allow_failure` on the selected CI command's ancestor objects must be literal `false` when present.\nUnmarked edits, other package scripts, other CI commands and unrelated configuration fields pass through the ordinary protected-write validation unchanged.\nThe plan digest binds these selections alongside the complete proposed bytes.\nSelected commands use literal arguments and fail-fast top-level `&&` chains; line breaks between commands are allowed only after `&&`.\nTokens are nonempty unquoted words or whole double-quoted literals, separated by whitespace.\nOnly ASCII spaces and tabs separate tokens; LF line breaks are allowed after `&&`, and leading/trailing spaces, tabs and LF are ignored.\nCarriage returns are unsupported anywhere in a command, including CRLF, quoted tokens and leading/trailing whitespace.\nUnicode whitespace remains literal; other ASCII control characters are unsupported.\nUnquoted `<` and `>` redirection, including attached forms, and unquoted leading executable `@` prefixes are unsupported in every command of a chain.\nDouble-quoted redirection characters and `@` remain literal, as do unquoted `@` argument characters.\nSingle quotes anywhere, mixed quoted/unquoted tokens and adjacent quoted fragments are unsupported in executable and argument positions.\nBackslashes and shell escaping are unsupported, including inside quoted arguments; use forward-slash paths and quotes for literal spaces.\nPercent and exclamation characters are unsupported in every script token, including double-quoted literals, because Windows can expand environment variables despite quotes.\nDollar signs and backticks are unsupported in every token, including double-quoted literals.\nUnquoted wildcard and bracket patterns, tilde expansion, variable and command substitution, brace expansion, extglobs and caret escaping are unsupported in executable and argument positions.\nQuoted wildcard, bracket and tilde characters remain literal, as do direct child-command argument arrays, which run without a shell.\nEvery decoded word is rejected if it contains `|`, `&`, `;` or a newline, including quoted arguments.\nQuotes do not make shell control syntax acceptable as an argument.\nFailure-masking operators (`||`, pipelines, separators, background execution and negation), unquoted shell comments, assignment prefixes, shell dispatch and substitutions fail with `ignored-check-failure` before any writes.\nShell dispatch includes `command`, `builtin`, shell executables, executable paths to those programs and their `.exe`/`.cmd`/`.bat` forms in every `&&` segment.\n`npm exec`/`npm x` and `npx` reject `-c` and `--call` modes, including attached option values; `env`, including `env -S`, remains unsupported.\nThis bounded grammar is a configuration safeguard, not an interpreter for arbitrary shell programs or a proof of a checker's implementation.\nThe identical maintained command must still pass the valid disposable control and reject the seeded violation with its diagnostic and nonzero exit through `rule-proof`.\nCI examples are unverified adapters until actual hosted execution is observed; local disposable proof does not establish a merge gate.\n\n## Control existing debt\n\nFix selected findings and keep the remaining findings visible.\nWhen immediate cleanup is unsuitable, propose a narrow record of known violations for the user to accept as temporary debt.\nPrefer the native tool's baseline or suppression mechanism where it exists.\nNew violations must fail, and fixed entries must leave the debt record so it shrinks as fixes land.\nDo not silently refresh a baseline to accept new failures.\nAn exception must record its reason, scope and removal condition.\nNever disable a whole rule or exclude a whole directory merely to obtain a green result.\nA changed-file-only check cannot establish a constraint that depends on the complete import graph or generated outputs.\n\nWhere native research found no baseline feature, use `schemas/baseline.schema.json` and `baseline check --repo <target> --baseline <repo-relative-file> --violations <file> --json`.\nSupply the complete current native violations as rule/path/key objects, preserving stable identities when lines move.\nKeep native execution failures separate from the violation list; unavailable analysis cannot establish an empty list.\nChecking reports retained debt and rejects new violations or entries whose violations were fixed.\n`--refresh` removes fixed entries but cannot accept new debt by itself.\nAdding one current violation requires audit only where shared language needs clarification.\nKeep proposals in scratch until confirmed or selected, then use the main procedure's review-before-apply and protected write boundary for project edits.\n\n## Discover the existing language and decisions\n\nRead the project's vocabulary source and relevant documents before asking naming questions.\nIf a root GLOSSARY-MAP.md exists, use it to find only the context glossaries relevant to the topic.\nOtherwise read the root GLOSSARY.md when present, respecting an existing source with another name or location.\nRead relevant architecture decision records, or ADRs, including context-scoped decisions and the design's decision history.\nMissing glossary or ADR filenames are not readiness failures.\nDo not create empty documents or directories upfront.\n\nFor an existing project without a glossary, derive candidate terms from documented domain concepts and relevant code.\nDistinguish established definitions from inferred meanings.\nCode shows current behaviour, not proof of the intended definition.\nPropose a glossary only when shared language would help.\nFor a new project, draft it when the user agrees on the first useful project-specific term.\n\n## Resolve meanings\n\nSurface conflicting definitions with file citations and a targeted question.\nFor example, if documentation defines cancellation for an entire order but code allows cancellation of one item, ask which meaning is intended.\nChallenge vague or overloaded terms by proposing precise canonical names.\nDistinguish a customer account from a user login when the project treats them as different concepts.\nUse concrete scenarios to test relationships and boundaries where unclear.\nResolve prerequisites first, giving a recommendation and trade-off with each decision question.\nGroup only independent questions into a small round and wait for answers.\n\nCapture resolved terms in the draft during the selected foundation session, without waiting for a second interview.\nGlossary proposals remain drafts until confirmed or selected.\nDo not infer approval from silence or an interrupted session.\nUse confirmed vocabulary in module names, interfaces, findings and proposed changes.\nDo not rename code automatically to match a draft.\nNaming migrations are separate selected changes with their own scope and verification.\n\n## Glossary format and contexts\n\nEach entry defines one project concept in one or two sentences.\nRecord its canonical name and conflicting synonyms where useful.\nInclude only project-specific domain concepts, not a generic programming dictionary.\nKeep feature requirements, implementation steps, storage details and coding rules in their appropriate sources.\nGroup terms only when natural clusters help.\nAdapt this minimal format to the project's existing conventions:\n\n```md\n# Ordering\n\nThe context that receives and tracks customer orders.\n\n## Language\n\n**Order**:\nA customer's request for a set of products.\n_Avoid_: Purchase, transaction\n\n**Customer**:\nA person or organisation that places orders.\n_Avoid_: Client, buyer\n```\n\nA domain context is an area with its own meanings and responsibilities, such as ordering or billing.\nUse one root glossary for one context.\nRecommend a new map only when genuinely different contexts need separate vocabulary, not merely to split a long document.\nThe same word can mean different things in different contexts.\nIf the relevant context is unclear, ask before defining the term.\nA map lists each context, links to its glossary and explains the relationships between contexts using domain language.\nKeep technical integration choices in the design or decision records.\n\n## Keep decisions separate\n\nSurface a conflict with an existing ADR or design decision explicitly, with its source and the reason for proposing reconsideration.\nUse the project's established decision location and format.\nAn existing design decision history can suffice.\nOffer a separate ADR only when the choice is hard to reverse, surprising without context and the result of a real trade-off.\nDo not create a record for every setup choice.\n\nWhen no format exists and a separate ADR is selected, use docs/adr/ with sequential names such as 0001-slug.md.\nInspect existing numbering and use the next number.\nCreate the directory only through a selected write when a real decision needs recording.\nThe minimal format is a short title followed by one to three sentences explaining the context, decision and why it was chosen.\nAdd status, considered options or consequences only when they add useful context.\nKeep proposed decisions separate from accepted ones.\n\nDone when: Relevant terms have confirmed meanings or named unresolved decisions, drafts contain only domain concepts, and meaningful technical decisions remain in their appropriate sources with no unselected project writes.\n# Architecture\n\nUse this reference when recommending module boundaries.\nStart with the reported area, stated next change, approved design and relevant history.\nExpand only where evidence leads.\nFor a new project, review expected changes against proposed boundaries instead of inventing history.\n\nCoupling means that a change needs knowledge of, or edits to, another part.\nCohesion means that code belongs together for a clear responsibility.\nA module is a logical unit with an interface and implementation, which may span several private files.\nA god file mixes unrelated responsibilities and becomes a shared target for different changes.\nLength alone does not establish that problem.\n\n## Principles to consider\n\nChoose principles only where project evidence supports them.\nNo single architecture style is required.\n\n| Principle | Practical rule | Source |\n|---|---|---|\n| Single responsibility and cohesion | Keep code together when it changes for the same business reason and separate unrelated reasons | [Single responsibility](https://blog.cleancoder.com/uncle-bob/2014/05/08/SingleReponsibilityPrinciple.html) |\n| Information hiding | Hide a changeable design decision behind an interface so callers do not depend on its representation | [Module decomposition](https://www.cs.lafayette.edu/~gexia/cs301/resources/parnas.html) |\n| Locality of change | Keep capability code and tests together where this reduces coordination across unrelated areas | [Vertical slices](https://www.jimmybogard.com/vertical-slice-architecture/) |\n| Explicit dependency direction | Define allowed dependencies and check forbidden imports and cycles across intended boundaries | [Dependency hierarchy](https://www.cs.lafayette.edu/~gexia/cs301/resources/parnas.html) |\n| Separate wiring from behaviour | Startup assembles modules while modules own business behaviour | [Composition roots](https://blog.ploeh.dk/2011/07/28/CompositionRoot/) |\n| Proportional deployment choices | A single deployed application can retain firm module boundaries | [Module boundaries](https://www.martinfowler.com/articles/microservice-trade-offs.html) |\n\nA deep module hides useful complexity behind a small interface.\nConsolidate tightly related shallow modules when removing their layer concentrates complexity in one place.\nSeparate unrelated responsibilities when their changes currently require shared edits.\nAvoid forwarding layers that add navigation without hiding useful complexity.\nOrganise ownership around domain knowledge rather than scattering one rule across load, validate and save phases.\nThese adaptations follow the pinned design and module guidance attributed in the package NOTICE.\n\n## What the audit inspects\n\n- Unrelated business responsibilities, or startup, UI, domain rules and persistence combined without clear internal boundaries.\n- Imports of private implementation, leaked representations and cycles across intended module boundaries.\n- Shared mutable state and multiple writers of canonical data.\n- Frequently changed files and pairs that repeatedly change together.\n- Central registries, broad utilities, shared type collections and wiring files that unrelated work repeatedly edits.\n- Tests requiring unrelated modules or asserting implementation details rather than a public contract.\n- Two representative planned changes, their write paths and the shared contracts they would modify.\n\nCollect dependency edges through the native analysis selected by the language research brief.\nResolve aliases, re-exports and package entry points.\nReport unsupported syntax, dynamic dependencies and unresolved imports as coverage limits.\nUnresolved imports do not demonstrate independence.\nPrefer an existing architecture checker to another dependency engine.\n\nFor history, record revision range, rename handling and exclusions for generated files, lockfiles and broad formatting changes.\nShow the supporting commits behind a finding.\nCo-change is distinct from observed merge conflicts.\nHistory may omit abandoned rebases and conflict resolutions.\n\n## Signals that are not violations\n\nSize, import counts and change frequency indicate where to investigate.\nThey do not prove a bad boundary.\nA large cohesive domain owner may be sound.\nA thin startup file may legitimately import many modules for wiring.\nA stable domain contract may have many callers.\nCo-change may reflect good cohesion, a mechanical commit or harmful coupling.\nInspect the reasons before recommending a split or consolidation.\nDo not infer conflicts or prescribe services from these signals.\n\n## Boundary rules\n\nName each module's responsibility, owned knowledge, public contract and private implementation.\nDefine allowed dependency direction and the scope of forbidden imports and cycles.\nCallers use the public interface and do not depend on private storage or representation.\nPrivate implementation may span files without exposing additional public entry points.\nKeep startup focused on assembly and put behaviour with its domain owner.\nRemove unnecessary shared write targets before serializing work.\nWhere sharing is real, enforce one writer and sequence unavoidable shared edits.\n\nReview two representative changes against proposed boundaries.\nFor example, a pricing-policy change and a notification-template change should remain inside their owners when the public contracts still suffice.\nCheck whether either also changes a shared type, registry or data representation.\nCompare declared paths and contract changes, then review semantic dependencies even when paths are disjoint.\nA script reports overlap evidence but cannot prove independence or predict every conflict.\nFewer unrelated shared edits are an expected benefit, not a measured reduction until actual evidence exists.\n\nRecord each proposed boundary's project-specific reason, source, scope, enforcement and exception policy.\nReuse existing design decisions and standards rather than duplicating their rules.\nPresent evidenced candidates and alternatives for selection before applying changes.\nVerification requires representative allowed and forbidden imports and compatible public behaviour, not file-count targets.\nUse rule-proof for a clean public-import control and independent private, alias or equivalent resolution bypass and cycle violations with the selected native check.\nThe Python equivalent-resolution fixture exercises an intermediate public-looking bridge to private implementation; proof does not establish every possible re-export or dynamic dependency.\nMaintain these coverage limits alongside the rule evidence.\n---\nname: lavish\ndescription: Turn complex or visual agent responses into rich, reviewable HTML artifacts (HTML files) the dark)\\\");\\n\\n  // Normalize any CSS color the browser produces (rgb, oklch, hsl, named, ...)\\n  // to [r, g, b, a] bytes via a 1x1 canvas, so parsing never breaks on modern\\n  // color syntaxes like DaisyUI's oklch() values.\\n  const paint = document.createElement(\\\"canvas\\\").getContext(\\\"2d\\\");\\n  function toRgba(color) {\\n    paint.clearRect(0, 0, 1, 1);\\n    paint.fillStyle = \\\"#000\\\";\\n    paint.fillStyle = color;\\n    paint.fillRect(0, 0, 1, 1);\\n    return paint.getImageData(0, 0, 1, 1).data;\\n  }\\n\\n  function compositeRgba(foreground, background) {\\n    const foregroundAlpha = foreground[3] / 255;\\n    const backgroundAlpha = background[3] / 255;\\n    const alpha = foregroundAlpha + backgroundAlpha * (1 - foregroundAlpha);\\n    if (alpha === 0) return [0, 0, 0, 0];\\n    return [\\n      (foreground[0] * foregroundAlpha + background[0] * backgroundAlpha * (1 - foregroundAlpha)) / alpha,\\n      (foreground[1] * foregroundAlpha + background[1] * backgroundAlpha * (1 - foregroundAlpha)) / alpha,\\n      (foreground[2] * foregroundAlpha + background[2] * backgroundAlpha * (1 - foregroundAlpha)) / alpha,\\n      alpha * 255,\\n    ];\\n  }\\n\\n  function pageIsDark() {\\n    // Trust the actually-rendered page background so this works with any theming\\n    // mechanism: prefers-color-scheme, a data-theme attribute, or plain CSS.\\n    const root = document.documentElement;\\n    const rootBackground = toRgba(getComputedStyle(root).backgroundColor);\\n    const bodyBackground = document.body ? toRgba(getComputedStyle(document.body).backgroundColor) : [0, 0, 0, 0];\\n    const [r, g, b, a] = compositeRgba(bodyBackground, rootBackground);\\n    if (a > 0) {\\n      return (0.2126 * r + 0.7152 * g + 0.0722 * b) / 255 < 0.5;\\n    }\\n    const colorScheme = getComputedStyle(root).colorScheme;\\n    if (colorScheme.includes(\\\"dark\\\") && !colorScheme.includes(\\\"light\\\")) return true;\\n    if (colorScheme.includes(\\\"light\\\") && !colorScheme.includes(\\\"dark\\\")) return false;\\n    return darkQuery.matches;\\n  }\\n\\n  const diagrams = [...document.querySelectorAll(\\\".mermaid\\\")].map((el) => ({ el, src: el.innerHTML }));\\n  let applied;\\n  let rendering = false;\\n  let queued = false;\\n  function queueRender() {\\n    queued = true;\\n    if (rendering) return;\\n    void render();\\n  }\\n  async function render() {\\n    rendering = true;\\n    try {\\n      while (queued) {\\n        queued = false;\\n        const theme = pageIsDark() ? \\\"dark\\\" : \\\"default\\\";\\n        if (theme === applied) continue;\\n        mermaid.initialize({ startOnLoad: false, theme, securityLevel: \\\"strict\\\" });\\n        for (const { el, src } of diagrams) {\\n          el.removeAttribute(\\\"data-processed\\\");\\n          el.innerHTML = src;\\n        }\\n        try {\\n          await mermaid.run({ nodes: diagrams.map((d) => d.el) });\\n        } catch (error) {\\n          console.error(\\\"Mermaid diagram render failed:\\\", error);\\n          return;\\n        }\\n        applied = theme;\\n      }\\n    } finally {\\n      rendering = false;\\n      if (queued) queueRender();\\n    }\\n  }\\n\\n  // First render once stylesheets are applied (no wrong-theme flash), then keep\\n  // the diagrams in sync with page-theme toggles and OS light/dark changes.\\n  if (document.readyState === \\\"complete\\\") queueRender();\\n  else window.addEventListener(\\\"load\\\", queueRender, { once: true });\\n  const themeObserver = new MutationObserver(queueRender);\\n  for (const el of [document.documentElement, document.body]) {\\n    if (!el) continue;\\n    themeObserver.observe(el, {\\n      attributes: true,\\n      attributeFilter: [\\\"data-theme\\\", \\\"class\\\", \\\"style\\\"],\\n    });\\n  }\\n  document.addEventListener(\\\"change\\\", queueRender, true);\\n  document.addEventListener(\\n    \\\"transitionend\\\",\\n    ({ propertyName }) => {\\n      if (propertyName === \\\"background-color\\\") queueRender();\\n    },\\n    true,\\n  );\\n  darkQuery.addEventListener(\\\"change\\\", queueRender);\\n</script>\"\n  cdn_urls:\n    mermaid: \"https://cdn.jsdelivr.net/npm/mermaid@11.15.0/dist/mermaid.esm.min.mjs\"\n  versions:\n    mermaid: 11.15.0\nrevision_marking:\n  use_when: \"Opt-in only: on a regeneration that answers the reviewer's feedback, declare what you changed so the browser can show a Revisions legend. Skip it on a first draft, and skip it when you rewrote the whole artifact - a legend that marks everything says nothing.\"\n  registry_snippet: \"<script type=\\\"application/json\\\" data-lavish-revisions>\\n[{ \\\"id\\\": \\\"r1\\\", \\\"label\\\": \\\"Round 1\\\", \\\"timestamp\\\": \\\"2026-01-01T10:00:00Z\\\", \\\"summary\\\": \\\"Tightened the pricing copy\\\" }]\\n</script>\\n<section data-lavish-revision=\\\"r1\\\">...the block you changed...</section>\"\n  how: \"Append one entry per round to the `data-lavish-revisions` JSON (oldest first, stable `id`s), and put `data-lavish-revision=\\\"<id>\\\"` on each block you actually edited or added. Pick a different theme from the list below only when the user asked for one or the content clearly calls for it.\",\"Set a nested section theme with `<section data-theme=\\\"night\\\">`.\",\"Prefer semantic colors such as `bg-base-100`, `bg-base-200`, `text-base-content`, `bg-primary`, `text-primary-content`, `alert-warning`, and `btn-primary` so themes remain readable.\",Avoid hardcoded Tailwind color names for text and surfaces unless the user asked for exact colors.,\"Use Tailwind responsive prefixes such as `sm:`, `md:`, `lg:`, and `xl:` for layout changes.\",\"Never `@apply` DaisyUI classes (such as `text-base-content/40`, `bg-base-200`, or `btn`) inside `<style type=\\\"text/tailwindcss\\\">` - the Tailwind browser runtime does not know them, and one unknown utility aborts the entire compile, leaving the page with no Tailwind styles at all. notes[2]: \"Compose DaisyUI components with Tailwind utilities for spacing, grid, flex, width, and typography.\",Prefer component classes over custom CSS for common UI.\nplaybook:\n  id: code\n  use_when: \"Render source code, code files, patches, PR diffs, and before/after code inside Lavish artifacts\"\n  choose[3]: \"Use this whenever an artifact shows source code: a snippet, full file, patch, PR diff, local change set, or before/after code.\",\"Use File for one code file, FileDiff for old/new versions or parsed patch metadata, and CodeView only when several files or diffs need coordinated navigation.\",\"Choose split layout for careful side-by-side review when width allows; choose unified layout when space is tight, changes are mostly additive, or mobile readability matters.\"\n  structure[3]: \"Place the path, language, and reason to inspect the code immediately before each rendered file or diff.\",\"Keep evidence close to each claim with file paths, line references, or annotations next to the relevant code.\",\"For multi-file changes, group files by user-facing area or task instead of dumping a raw patch in repository order.\"\n  design_rules[4]: \"Rendering MUST use @pierre/diffs, not hand-rolled <pre> blocks or another diff library. This verified no-build standalone HTML snippet renders one file and one split diff from esm.sh:\\n```html\\n<div id=\\\"file\\\"></div>\\n<div id=\\\"diff\\\"></div>\\n<script type=\\\"module\\\">\\n  import { File, FileDiff } from \\\"https://esm.sh/@pierre/diffs@1.2.10?bundle\\\";\\n\\n  const theme = { light: \\\"github-light\\\", dark: \\\"github-dark\\\" };\\n  const options = { theme, themeType: \\\"dark\\\", overflow: \\\"wrap\\\" };\\n  const oldFile = {\\n    name: \\\"src/greeting.ts\\\",\\n    contents: \\\"export function greet(name: string) {\\\\n  return \\\\\\\"Hello \\\\\\\" + name;\\\\n}\\\\n\\\\nconsole.log(greet(\\\\\\\"Lavish\\\\\\\"));\\\\n\\\",\\n  };\\n  const newFile = {\\n    name: \\\"src/greeting.ts\\\",\\n    contents: \\\"export function greet(name: string) {\\\\n  return \\\\\\\"Hello, \\\\\\\" + name + \\\\\\\"!\\\\\\\";\\\\n}\\\\n\\\\nconsole.log(greet(\\\\\\\"Lavish\\\\\\\"));\\\\n\\\",\\n  };\\n\\n  new File(options).render({\\n    containerWrapper: document.querySelector(\\\"#file\\\"),\\n    file: newFile,\\n  });\\n\\n  new FileDiff({ ...options, diffStyle: \\\"split\\\" }).render({\\n    containerWrapper: document.querySelector(\\\"#diff\\\"),\\n    oldFile,\\n    newFile,\\n  });\\n\\n</script>\\n```\",Pick a Shiki theme pair that matches the artifact's DaisyUI or Tailwind direction and light or dark mode; replace the GitHub pair above when the page is not GitHub-like.,\"Use FileDiff diffStyle: \\\"split\\\" for side-by-side review and diffStyle: \\\"unified\\\" for stacked reading; keep overflow: \\\"wrap\\\" unless horizontal alignment is essential.\",\"Use @pierre/diffs line annotations, selections, and headers when calling out specific lines so notes stay attached to code.\"\n  pitfalls[4]: \"Do not render code as static screenshots, plain <pre> blocks, or markdown pasted into HTML.\",Do not choose an arbitrary default Shiki theme that clashes with the page palette or dark mode.,\"Do not show huge unrelated files when a focused render range, parsed patch file, or grouped summary would be clearer.\",Do not separate a claim from the code lines that prove it.\n  lavish_notes[3]: \"A Lavish code artifact should make each file, hunk, and relevant line easy to annotate precisely.\",\"When a user action should trigger a fix, queue prompts that name the file path, line range, and desired change.\",\"If the artifact combines code with a plan, table, or comparison, read those playbooks too and keep @pierre/diffs responsible for the code surface.\"\nplaybook:\n  id: input\n  use_when: \"Must be used when the agent needs to collect user input on decisions, choices, preferences, triage, scope, or other structured feedback from within the artifact\"\n  choose[4]: \"Use this when the user needs to select, tune, triage, annotate, or edit a structured choice.\",Use controls for decisions the user can make faster visually than by writing a prompt.,\"Use an opt-in tracked batch when the agent must preserve completeness across a multi-item decision, such as findings to fixes, constraints to implementation, or recommendations to follow-up work.\",Use plain annotations when the artifact only needs open-ended feedback.\n  structure[5]: \"Make each decision surface visible: what is being chosen, what the options mean, and what happens next.\",\"For a tracked batch, give every candidate item a short, stable, visible ID and let the reviewer select or disposition items with editable native controls.\",Keep reversible selection state local in the artifact until the user explicitly submits that question.,Pair each question with a Submit or Queue answer control that sends exactly one prompt for the final answer.,Show selected state separately from queued state so the user trusts what will be sent back.\n  design_rules[10]: \"Native controls - radios, checkboxes, text inputs, selects, textareas, buttons, options, labels, disclosure summaries, and contenteditable regions - are interactive automatically: clicks toggle, focus, and type instead of annotating, so they do not need data-lavish-action. Build choice and option UIs from these whenever you can.\",\"For reversible choices, do not call window.lavish.queuePrompt() from radio change handlers or option click handlers. Those handlers should only update local selected state.\",Use a per-question form submit or explicit Queue answer button to read the current values and call window.lavish.queuePrompt() exactly once for the final answer.,Put data-lavish-action only on custom (non-native) elements that should act like a feedback control - typically a styled div or span you made clickable - so Lavish does not annotate it and shows a pointer cursor instead.,Use data-lavish-question on a question wrapper or pass queueKey when multiple pre-send updates should replace the prior unsent answer for the same question.,\"Pass options such as tag, text, selector, target, data, queueKey, or element when they help the agent understand exactly what the user chose.\",\"For a tracked batch, queue the final selected set once in a concise, bounded data.items array; include each item's stable ID, concise label, and requested disposition, and explicitly tell the agent to account for every submitted ID before reporting completion.\",Call window.lavish.sendQueuedPrompts() only when the control should immediately send committed feedback instead of waiting for the user to press Send to Agent.,Make queued prompts specific enough that the agent can act without asking a follow-up question.,Keep native browser controls accessible and readable on mobile.\n  pitfalls[4]: \"Do not queue one prompt per radio change, checkbox toggle, dropdown change, or choice-button click when the user can still change their mind.\",Do not create controls whose queued prompt is unclear or too vague to execute.,Do not hide the difference between selected locally and queued for the agent.,Do not require interaction for content the user only needs to read.\n  lavish_notes[7]: Lavish is strongest when the artifact becomes a focused review surface and not just a static page.,\"A native single-choice question should submit the final value: `<form data-lavish-question=\\\"plan\\\" onsubmit=\\\"event.preventDefault(); const choice = new FormData(event.currentTarget).get('plan'); if (choice) window.lavish.queuePrompt('Use the ' + choice + ' plan', { tag: 'choice', text: 'Plan: ' + choice, element: event.currentTarget, data: { question: 'plan', answer: choice } });\\\"><label><input type=\\\"radio\\\" name=\\\"plan\\\" value=\\\"Starter\\\"> Starter</label><label><input type=\\\"radio\\\" name=\\\"plan\\\" value=\\\"Pro\\\"> Pro</label><button type=\\\"submit\\\">Queue this answer</button></form>`.\",\"A tracked batch should submit the final selected set once: <form data-lavish-question=\\\"tracked-review\\\" onsubmit=\\\"event.preventDefault(); const selected = [...event.currentTarget.querySelectorAll('input[name=items]:checked')].map((input) => ({ id: input.value, label: input.dataset.label, disposition: input.dataset.disposition })); if (selected.length) window.lavish.queuePrompt('Act on every selected item and return an item-by-item receipt. Account for every submitted ID before reporting completion.', { tag: 'tracked-batch', text: 'Apply ' + selected.length + ' selected review items', element: event.currentTarget, data: { items: selected } });\\\"><label><input type=\\\"checkbox\\\" name=\\\"items\\\" value=\\\"R-03\\\" data-label=\\\"Preserve rollback behavior\\\" data-disposition=\\\"must-address\\\"> R-03 — Preserve rollback behavior</label><label><input type=\\\"checkbox\\\" name=\\\"items\\\" value=\\\"R-08\\\" data-label=\\\"Reuse the existing error surface\\\" data-disposition=\\\"must-address\\\"> R-08 — Reuse the existing error surface</label><button type=\\\"submit\\\">Queue selected items</button></form>\",\"The agent's tracked-batch completion receipt must give every submitted ID exactly one outcome: addressed with concrete evidence, deferred with a reason, or rejected with a reason.
